JAPANESE EMBASSY
WASHINGTON
February 29, 1940
Between February 15 and February
24, special restrictions on shipping
were applied to that section of the
Shantung Peninsula coast which lies
between Weihaiwei and Tsingtao. The
special restrictions, which were im-
posed in connection with measures for
the extermination of piracy, forbade
all shipping to ap roach or depart
from any point in that coastal section
exclusive of the ports of Weihaiwei
and Tsingtao.

I have the honor to refer to the report from this
Consulate dated February 16, 1940 entitled "Shipping
894.85
in the Japanese Empire During January 1940", in which it
5
will be noted that new secrecy steps apparently are being
5
2
taken with regard to commercial ship construction figures.
For the past three months the only available ship
construction figures have been expressed in ratios to
the base month of December 1938, which was given the
tandard index number of "1o0o". Upon computation the
FILED
could be ascertained, but it was feared this was the first
21940
stop in the gradual elimination of all official published
construction figures. This impression appeared correct,
F/A
for the February 1940 issue of the periodical Kaiun
(Marine Affairs) gives only the ratios for October and
November 1939, which were included in the report from
this office dated January 18, 1940 ontitled "shipping in
the

=== Page 10 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
the Japanese Empire During December 1939".
On the subject of secrecy it should be added that
no information regarding orders for new tonnage has been
published recently and it is feared this trend may even
extend to official lists of commercial launchings.
In the matter of construction figures one reason
may be advanced. It is that the materials shortage is
so serious the entire construction program has been
greatly hampered in its objective of a merchant fleet of
7,500,000 tons. It would appear that to conceal this
from the Japanese public and others the secrecy ban has
been invoked. Meanwhile the Consulate will attempt
through unofficial sources to obtain any construction
data that may be available.
The tendency has been noted for sources of shipping
information to be gradually restricted, for there is a
marked recent decrease in the amount of published Japanese
shipping news. Japanese sources may not be approached
directly, since it is embarrassing to them, so foreign
informants must be even more heavily relied upon. In
many cases they are encountering similar difficulties.
It is personally known to this Consulate that a local
branch of a steamship agency inoorporated in Japan, but
handling Norwegian lines, is unable to secure the daily
Kobe Shipping Exchange Bulletin, long ago denied to all
foreign sources. The reason advanced for denying this
information to the agency is that it is a "foreign firm",
although it has Japanese capital and some Japanese direo-
tors.
In order partially to safeguard the remaining shipping
souroos

=== Page 11 === (confidence 0.96)
- 3 -
sources of the Consulate it has been deemed best in the
future to treat the entire source list of its monthly
shipping reports as confidential.
Respectfully yours,
Wn e Offeld. 7.
Wm. C. Affeld, Jr.
American Vice Consul

I have the honor to refer to confidential despatch
No. 224 from this Consulate dated February 16, 1940
concerning difficulties experienced in reporting on
shipping activities, in which it was stated that secrecy
measures had been instituted with regard to Japanese
commercial ship construction statistics.
During the past month extensive inquiries have been
MAY
conducted by this office with a view toward obtaining
FILE
fairly reliable unofficial commercial ship construction
figures from various shipping sources. None of the Con
sulate's contacts have been able to supply the informa-
tion, and the local Lloyd's shipping representative has
F/A
confessed to me his inability to gather any statistics
since December 1939. He stated that during the latter
month he was informed by the appropriate Japanese authori-
ties that no further construction information would be
supplied to him. Since Great Britain is no longer making
ship

=== Page 16 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
ship construction statements available regarding its own
building, the local Lloyd's representative has been un-
able to influence the authorities to modify their stand.
The March issue of Kaiun (Marine Affairs) carried
no ship construction figures whatsoever, and in the
February issue, as previously renorted, only ratios based
on old figures were made available.
The Lloyd's representative confirms the view pre-
viously expressed by this office, that the shipbuilding
program is bojin down so badly because of material and
labor shortages, that the Government has decided to con-
ceal the fact, and that the merchant fleet program is
not being attained. Newspaper articles, formerly admit-
ting a 20 per cent delay in the bu:lding schedule, now
The
Lloyd's representative declares that these public ad-
missions of delay are themselves erroneous, and that the
shipbuilding program is fully 50 per cent behind schedule.
Respectfully yours,
Wan affed 9
Wm. C. Affeid, Jr.

Japanese Companies Engaged in South American Services
After an investigation of Japanese world wide ship-
ping services it has been learned that there are four
•stablished steamship lines engaged in shipping services
to Latin American ports. These companies are Kawasaki
Kisen Kabushiki Kaisha, Nippon Yusen Kaisha, Osaka Shosen
Kaisha, and Yamashita Kisen Kaisha. These companies
have reported schedules which vary from stops on world
wide routes to those devoted exclusively to the Latin
American carrying trade. A short summary of the services
provided by each company has been obtained through direct
contact with the steamship lines concerned, and the
rosults are as follows:
Services of the Kawasaki Kisen Kabushiki Kaisha
The monthly round-the-world cargo service of the
Kawasaki Kisen Kabushiki Kaisha is at present suspended;
one of the reasons being given by the company for this
suspension is the unsatisfactory and dangerous maritime
conditions now existing as a result of the European war.
However, the West Coast - Central and South American
cargo and mail service of the company continues every
two months with the use of three vessels, aggregating
19,1ll tons. The itinerary of this route starts in
Japan, proceeds to Los Angeles, thence to Manzanillo,
Corinto, Punta Arenas, Balboa, Guayaquil, Callao, Mollondo,
Arica, Valparaiso, and thence returns by the same route.
This latter service was established in l936.
sorv1ees

=== Page 21 === (confidence 0.97)
- 3 -
Services of the Nippon Yusen Kaisha
The Nippon Yusen Kaisha has conducted since 1926
a monthly passenger, cargo and mail service to the
West Coast of South America. At present there are three
ships employed in this service with a total tonnage of
28,038. The ships follow the usual Pacific crossing
route to the West Coast via Hawaii, and thence proceed
to Manzanillo, Balboa, Buenaventura, Callao, Pisco,
Mollendo, Arica, Iquique, and terminate at Valparaiso.
On homeward trips the ships retrace the same route.
Sorvices of the Osaka Shosen Kaisha
The Osaka Shosen Kaisha has had an old established
round-the-world service, which has always called at
Latin American ports, and at present this service is
conduoted w1th two now hips, each of 13,000 gross tons,
with one sailing every two months. The vessels carry
passengers, cargo and mail from Japan to Latin America
via South Africa. The ports of call in Latin America
are Rio de Janoiro, Santos, Montevideo and Buenos Aires.
At Buonos Aires the ships retrace their course up the
East Coast of South America and proceed to Japan by
way of the Panama Canal.
A new east-bound service of the Osaka Shosen Kaisha
was inaugurated in 1939, utilising three ships of
26,521 total gross tonnage. They carry passengers,
cargo and mail on a two months' sohedule from Japan to
Los Angeles, thence through the Panama Canal, atopping
at various ports on the Bast Coast of South America
until Buenos Aires is reached. The homeward journey
retraees this trip.
2orvi00e

=== Page 22 === (confidence 0.96)
- 4 -
Services of the Yamashita Kisen Kaisha
At the end of 1937 the Yamashita Kisen Kaisha es-
tablished a Far East - New York - South America service
to which it has gradually added vessels until there are
now seven ships, aggregating 46,866 tona, engaged on the
route. The service is a monthly one consisting of mail
and cargo. It proceeds to New York via Los Angeles and
the Panama Canal. From New York the ships go down the
East Coast of South America, stopping at Pernambuco,
Bahia, Rio de Janeiro, Santos, Montevideo and Buenos Aires.
The home voyage is via the Panama Canal without a re-
turn to New York.
Conclusion
It will be seen that as a whole Japanese shipping
sorvices to Latin America are of comparatively recent
origin. At present, in view of the European war, the
steamship companies are unwilling to risk their vessels
on dangerous routes. Thus, many comparatively new ships
are being utilized on the various Latin American services.

=== Page 32 === (confidence 0.96)
-1-
The press reports that two of Japan's largest and
most modern steamers, the "brasil Maru" and the
"Argentina Maru", have been withdrawn from service to
and from t.he Ameriaas, and that they will be substituted
by smaller oraft such as the "Buenos Aires Maru" and
"Montevideo Maru". In newspaper articles on the subjeot
it is stated that those interested in passages or cargo
shipments are given evasive replies by the agents for
the Japanese line. While no infornation is being given
to the public as to th: withdrawal, a prominent employee
of the ships agents confi dentially inforned a reliable
Amerioan shipping broker, who cooperates closely with
this office, that the vessels mentioned above were in
faot being recalled to Japan, and that they would not
soon return to Brazil.
Apparently Japan feels that almost anything may
ocour in the near future, and that she wishes her newest
and largest merchant vessels to be in waters where they
can be protected by the Japanese fleet. By the same
token, should any of her boats be sacrifioed as a result
of future international developments, she would prefer
that such losses be confined to smaller, seoond class
ships.
Looal newspapers express the belief that the reoall
may be a direot result of the tri-partite agreement
between Japan, Italy and Germany.

My dear Mr. Berle:
As of possible interest to you, this Bureau has been
furnished with the following extract from the April 7,
1941 editlon of the "Pecific Shipper" relative to the
recent tendency of Japanese tankers to eliminate the
passage of the Panama Canal.
"Somewhat unexnectedly, the Japanese Government took
the first forthright step toward dispensing with the
Paname Canal. Its Shipping Control Division directed
that none of its tankers should load hereafter on the
Gulf of Mexico or at Atlantic or Caribbean ports that
would necessitate a transit of the Canal, but should
secure all their cargoes in California or other Pacific
areas. Despatches from Tokyo cite the potential saving
in Canal tolls, but the trade interpreted economy of
shipping operations as the real reason behind the move.
The supply of tankers has been inadequate throughout
the world ever since the war began. Regardless of costs,
the tankers available can deliver more oil to Japan by
excluding all but the shortest voyages."
From the reliable confidential source, 1t was also
ascertained that there has been a large increase in the
number of Japanese tankers loading at San Francisco in
the immediate past.
Sincerely yours,
J. E. HOOVER

Navy Depertment, Waehington, D. C.

one hundred and eighty third class passensers.

vessel a speed of 21 knots.

SUBJECT:
Difficulties in Reporting Upon
Japanese Shipping.
33

THE SECRETARY OF STATE.
91 16!

FAR EASTERN AFFAIHS
A
WASHINGTON.

SIR:
JUI
1546.
FA
I have the honor to refer to this Consulate's con-
ne Desk
1557311554
dential despatches noa. 224 and a38 of February 16,
artment of Stato
MERCIAL AFFAIRS
YUL
8.1941
VISq
vailable sources in connection with reports upon
UN
Japanese shipping and to report that the availability of
shipping news channels has continued to decline markedly.
The official policy of restriction upon all varieties
of shipping information has vecome increasingly effective.
As the result of Government propaganda regarding the
FIL
patriotism of withholding even the most elementary facts
1941
and the recent fillip given this trend by the National
Defence Security Law, an effective wedge has been driven
between the Japanese and foreign residents in shipping
matters. So strong is the Government influence in this
field that any foreigner would hesitate before posing to
PS/HH
a Japanese the simplest query regarding shipping matters
outside

=== Page 76 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
outside of the actual sailing date of a specific passenger
vessel.
The amount of printed sources covering shipping has
seriously declined and the actual content, when available,
is found to be deficient in many respects. For example,
facts regarding charter rates are eliminated. Statistics,
apparently from the very fact that they are statistics, are
no longer printed. Tables showing distribution of Japa-
nese tonnage throughout the world are beginning to be
delayed in their appearance. It is felt in the near
future, whatever may be thought of their present value and
the accuracy of the figures set forth, that all tonnage
distribution figures will disappear. Available tonnage
distribution figures have been lessened in value for some
time, since ships of the ippon Yusen haisha and of the
Osaka Shosen Kaisha are not permitted to be included.
The president of the ippon usen haisha in addressing a
stockholders meeting several months ago is reported to
have referred to the fact that, as much as he would like
to do so, new N. Y. K. construction and the tonnage
figures of the company could not be mentioned.
Naval ship launchings ceased being reported in even
fragmentary form several months ago and data regarding
commercial launchings are now exceedingly incomplete, with
good prospects thatthis information shortly will be com-
pletely withheld.
The Japanese authorities are now reliably stated to be
questioning at least once a week all Japanese firms connec-
ted with shipping as to whether they have given information
of any kind, directly or indirectly, to foreign concerns.
40

=== Page 77 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
At present the Consulate does not receive as a
regular subscriber any Japanese ship ing puulication, since
this is not permitted by the authorities. Time after time
the Consulate has endeavored without success to obtain
such publications. The sole  rinted matter available to the
Consulate is the daily press, both nglish and Japanese.
Because of newspaper controls it is believed that the inter-
ested authorities can fully determine what items of ship-
ping may be published. ..ore and more these official news
reports tend to be misleadine and past experience must be
heavily drawn upon to interpret them.
Japanese publications ar dif:icult to secure. The
monthly shipping magazine KAIUN (Marine Affairs) up to now
has been bought at a local bookstore, but it is expected
that it will be unavailable next month, since the current
issue was "sold out" when the Consulate made application
and a borrowed copy was secured only after great difficulty.
There is still one weekly Japanese shipping publication
available to the Consulate through a local source. How
ever, when it comes to the translating of shipping material
from Japanese sources into English, the reluctance of the
Japanese consular staff can be sensed, since the feeling
now appears to be present that material in Japanese is
not really intended for foreigners.
Personal contacts in foreign shipping circles are
the heart of the Consulate's reports on Japanese shipping.
Suoh contacts render great assistance in the interpretation
of press items and in making available information not
otherwise obtainable. However, many of the best of such
past

=== Page 78 === (confidence 0.97)
-4-
past sources of information have left Japan through the
decline of business necessit ting the closing of their
offices. Those who remain have litile active business
and this, combined with the strict withholding of all infor-
mation to foreigners, has meant that their sources, except
 thin very narrow limits, are growing more meagre.
The only sug estion this consulate can advance
partially to meet this situation is that increased atten-
tion within local limits could be paid to Japanese ship-
ping matters by all consular offices in order that no
available items vorthy of attention would be missed.
This despatch is intended to emphasize to the Depart-
ment the increasin: difficulties in respect to a phase
of consular reporting on which this office has always placed
great importance and which the Department has in various
instructions to this office also directed should be given
the closest attention. The Consulate desires to assure
the Department that it will continue to pay the closest
attention to Japanese shipping.
Respectfully yours,
Bne sonim
Samuel Sokobin,
American Consul.

=== Page 82 === (confidence 0.99)
MEMORANDUM
G.S.Walden,
July 3,1941
ilding
Shipments-Japan
Dear Mr.Walden:-
In the course of the last few months,there has been a oon
siderable decrease in the number of Japanese ships calling at East
Coast U.S.A. ports to load for Japan,and since it is apparent that none
of the Japanese Lines are scheduling their vessels for East Coast and
Gulf loadings beyond the first half of this month,I outline the posi-
tion as gathered from my conversations with individuals conneoted with
the Lines below:-
N.Y.K.
This organization ordinarily has one or two sailings
monthly from North Atlantic ports to base ports in Japan. Their
"Azuma Maru" sailed from New York for Japan on June 24. They have no
July sailin and, while it was expected that the "Nozima Maru" would
be available in North Atlantic ports ana the Gulf late August, that
vessel apparently is being held in Japan or will be divertea to another
trade. No further sailings are scheduled.
O.S.K.
This Line usually has one or two sailings monthly. Their
"Tozan Maru" is due in New York July 8, carrying about 62o0 tons of
inwara cargo.The "Tozan Maru" is under orders to unload the inward
cargo expeditiously with' a definite view of being prepared to sail full
or partly full,not later than July 13. No further sailings are sche-
aulea.
KOKUSAI
This Company orainarily has one and occasionally two
sailings monthly from North Atlantic ports to Japan. Their "Kiyosumi
Maru" is due in New Yor! July 7, carrying e substantial inward shipment.
Instructions have been received to unload such inward cargo expeai-
tiously and to load as much outward cargo as cuickly as possible with
definite view of sailing from New York not later than the 13th. No
rurther sailings are scheduled.
KAWASAKI
This Company usually schedules one sailing monthly. Their
"Norfolk Maru" is due in New York July 6 carrying inwara cargo. It is
expectea to proceed via Baltimore,instructions being,to clear from the
North Atlantio not later than July 13. No further sailings are sohe-
duled.
.1.

=== Page 83 === (confidence 0.99)
7/3/41
-2-
G.S.Walden
MITSUI
This Line usually has one or two sailings monthly. The "Amagasin
Maru" sailed from New York yesterday for U.S.Gulf ports. There are no
further sailings scheduled.
YAMASHITA LINE
This Company carries inwara cargo to New York and then proceeds
via South America to Japan. They ordinarily are not interested in
Japanese cargo out of New York since it is their desire to load South
American products at Rio,Santos,Montevideo ana Buenos Aires for base
ports,Japan. The "Yamazato Maru" is due in New York tomorrow and the
"Yamaura" is due in New York July S. These ships are expected to dis-
charge cargo at Baltimore and philadelphia,return to New York and pro-
ceed from here about July lo. The chances are that sailings will be
advancea and they will clear New York about July 13,foregoing the
River Plate calls. No further sailings are scheduled.
Despite the fact that we have been most anxious in the course o
the last few weeks to provide for our Yokohama stock reguirements out of
the Gulf,it has been impossible to secure space from Japanese Lines, there
being very clear indications of the inability of Japanese Lines to commit
themselves beyond the middle of July-all sailings thereafter said to have
been cancelled.
It is noteworthy that the "Kirashima Maru" and the "Tokai Maru
are due to depart from New Orleans about July ll,carrying a total of about
15,000 wood barrels and 2o00 bulk tons of Lubricants for account of Mitsu-
bishi and Mitsui & Company. Reyorts disclose that the ships must clear the
canal by July 20. Mitsui's "Amagasin Maru" is also enroute to the Gulf to
load some Brazil cotton and other cargo with the intention of being out o
the Gulr by July 13.
All of this is so inaicative of further movements on the part
the Japanese Government. It is said that there is an effort on the part a
Japan to increase the number of sailings from our West coast ports to Jap
sre Any intention to keep Japanese ships in the pacific,is not borne out
by incuiries placed before Japanese Lines for space to accommodate oils f
stook account which we might be able to secure from the West Coast rather
than the Gulf. No Japanese Shipping Lines are prepared to enter into any
rurther arrangements with us at present:
Yours truly,
(SIGNED) R F VAUGHAN

WASHINGTON 12 Juy, 1941.
CONEDENITIAL
Subject:
Japanese M TOKAI MARU.
Reference:
Summary of Information:
On 1 July, 194l, subject vessel while at the port of Baltimore,
requested permission from the Customs to take on board 38 drums of
lubricating oil for ship's use. The need for this oil was discussed
and it developed that the TOKAI MARU already had on board 5,000
gallens of lubricating oil. The ship was ready to sail, and when
the master was asked for a statement of the ship's daily lubricant
use, the 38 barrels of oil, which had already been put on board,
were transferred ashore and the order was cancelled.
On 15 January, 1941, Captain Biichi Sanda and 4th engineer
Kense Yamarouti of this vessel were arrested in the Canal Zone
for unlawful use of cameras and were fined $25.00 each.

=== Page 115 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No.l to Despatoh No.5073
dated July 26, 1941, from American
Embassy, Rio de Janeiro, Brazil.
COPY
"Rio de Janeiro, 25 de Julho de 1941.
"Illmo. Sr. Dr. Costa Rego,
M.D. Director do "Correio da Manhã",
Rio de Janeiro.
A maioria dos jornaes têm publicado noticias sobre possi-
bilidades de interrupção da navegação japonesa para o Brasil.
Para restabelecer a verdade, dirijo-me a essa illustrada
redação, pedindo-lhe o obsequio de publicar a presente carta,
que põe a questão nos seus verdadeiros termos.
Não ha, em absoluto, até agora, determinação alguma no
sentido de ser suspensa a navegação japonesa para o Brasil e
tanto nós, aqui, como lá, a alta direcção da nossa Companhia
em Osaka, estamos faz endo todos os esforços para cue ella não
soffra interrupqão. Posso affirmar-lhe que o Japão não sus-
penderá, de forma alguma, assim, o seu commercio com a America
do Sul, de um modo especial com o Brasil, a que nos ligam tão
grandes interesses communs.
Feitas estas ligeiras considerações, desejo tornar bem
patente, por intermedio do "Correio da Lianhã", ao publico bra-
sileiro, em geral, e aos nossos commitentes e amigos em parti-
oular, o seguinte:
a) 0 fechamento do Canal do Panamá não tem importancia vi-
tal para a nossa navegação e o nosso intercambio com-
mercial com a America do Sul.
b) Atravessando o Estreito de lagalhães ou melhor ainda,
cruzando o Cabo llorn, a viagem pode ser realizada per-
feitamente, sem maiores difficuldades.
c) Nossos navios preferiam a viagem pelo caminho de Panamá
porque assim desembarcavam, no Brasil, e embarcavam para
o Japão, grande cópia de mercadorias, nos portos de
Belém, Recife e Bahia.
d) A viagem pelo extremo sul do Continente nos levará,
apenas a tomar como ponto terminal de nossa linha o Rio
de Janeiro, fazendo, aqui, transbordo das meroadorias
que devem prosseguir para o norte e dando assim, car-
gas a outras companhias de navegação, inclusive o
Lloyd Brasileiro, com quem mantemos desde ha tempos,
um convenio legalmente assignado.
e) Em abono as nossas affirmagões iniciaes, tenho, emfim,
a dizer-lhe que, em meiados de Agosto proximo, deve
chegar ao Rio de Janeiro o "Buenos Aires Maru" e que
a seguir, outros navios japoneses aqui chegarão.
Esperando que V.S. divulgue a presente, subscrevo-me.,
Amo.Atto. Obrg.
SOC. DE NAVEGAÇÃO OSAKA DO BRASIL LTDA.
(O.S.K.LINE). Sub-AgenTeS: OSAKA SYOSEN KAISYA
(Assig.)
TAKEO SATO,
Gerente.

=== Page 116 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No.2 to Despatoh No.5073
dated July 26, 194l, from Amerioan
Embassy, Rio de Janeiro, Brazil.
TRANSLATION
"Rio de Janeiro, July 25, 1941.
"Dr. Costa Rego,
Director of the "Correio da Manhã",
Rio de Janeiro.
The majority of newspapers have published statements
concerning the possibility of interruption of Japanese
navigation to Brazil.
To re-establish the truth, I ask you the favor to
publish the following, which definite explains the situa-
tion:
Up to the present there has been no definite decision
as regards the suspending of Japanese shipping to Brazil,
and we here as well as the high officials of our Company
in Osaka are doing everything in our power to the end that
the service will not be interrupted. We can affirm that
Japan will not in any form suspend its commerce with South
America, particularly Brazil, with which country we have
such great common interests.
In connection with the above comments, I wish to make
it clear, through the "Correio da Manhã", to the Brazilian
public in general, our friends and those with whom we have
commitments that:
a) The closing of the Panamá Canal is not of vital
importanoe to our shipping and our trade with South
America.
b) Using the Strait of Magellan or, still better, going
around Cape Horn, the voyage can be made without
great difficulty.
c) Our vessels would prefer to make the trip via the
Panamá Canal because thus we could unload Japanese
goods in Brazil and ship Brazilian goods to Japan
at the ports of Belém, Recife and Bahia.
d) The voyage via the extreme South of the Continent
would oblige us to make Rio de Janeiro our last
stop in Brazil, transshipping merohandise whioh is
destined for the North by other companies, inoluding
the Lloyd Brasileiro, with which oompany w have long
since maintained a legal agreement.
e) To substantiate our earlier statements, I can say that
in the middle of next August there should arrive in
Rio de Janeiro the S.S. "Buenos Aires Maru", and that
other Japanese boats will arrive in due course.
Hoping

=== Page 117 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
Hoping that you will publish the above, I am
Very truly yours,
SOC. DE NAVEGAÇÃO OSAKA DO BRASIL LTDA.
(O.S.K.LINE). Sub-Agents: OSAKA SYOSEN KAISYA
(Signed)
TAKEO SATO,
Manager.

our Principuls in rokyo, which in it trimalation readyns follows:
"ALL BOOKINGS OF PERUVIAN COTTON FOn TIHL AKAGI ARU AND THE
FOLLOWING VESSLLS ARE TO BE CANCELLED. THE SPACE FOR COTTON
ON ALL TILLSE SHIPS ..ILL BE NEWLY CONMITTED IN JAPAN BY THE

Since we have to undorstand that the above inatructions fron our
Principals is the result of control of cotton shipments to Japan by
the Committee ospecially orgnnized for this purpose under the auspices
of the Japanese Governnent, we have no alternative, ns a matter of
compulsion, but to follow the aforementioned order and hereby cancel all
the bookings of Peruvian Cotton made with you for our company's vessel
concerned, viz:
"Akngi Maru" (August niling)
"Heiyo Maru" (August/September sniling)
"Sanuki Maru" (Soptember sniling - booking ex "Snkito Mnru")
"Awata Maru" (September sailing)

question and we would not fail to recommend you to negotiate with your
buyers in Japan in this connection, as wo are given to understand that
all Japanese cotton importers are mombers of the aforosaid Cotton
Control Committee and have therefore partidipated or were at least
represented in the Conference, in which the above step was taken. The
Japanese importers are to apply for cotton space direct to the Committee
in future.

Norfolk Maru was sailing from there July 11 with 22 cases

United States. Mr. Crane later called to say that he had

Embassy on the 22 boxes on the Norfolk Maru were inviolate.

July 12,1941
With reference to the attached memorandum
of conversation in regard to the Japanese
merchant vessel Norfolk Maru, Mr. Wallace of
IN at the request of FE Inquired of the
Treasury Department as to the reasons for the
reported delay'of this ship. According to
Mr. Wallace, the Treasury stated that upon
the basis of reports from the Office of Mili-
tary Intelligence to the effect that the crew
of the Norfolk Maru together with the crew
of another unnamed Japanese vessel in Baltimore
harbor were intending to commit sabotage on
the Panama Canal upon their return voyage to
Japan, the Customs had examined the interior
of the Norfolk Maru without results and were
now engaged in sending divers over the side to
find out whether any detachable explosives
had been fixed to the outside of the vessel.
It was expected, however, that the ship would
not be unduly delayed.
It is suggested that Mr. Schuler be
authorized to reply to Mr. Okumura of the
Japanese Embassy by telephone along lines as
follows:
With respect to the delayed departure

the report he had concerning the fact that other flagships
were being enabled to come through. I said that if this
were in fact the case, I assumed that they must be merchant
ships carrying material required for the United States de-
fense program and I said I was of course sure the Amoas-
sador would agree that it was entirely logical to suppose
that if any exceptions were made, ships carrying cargoes
urgently required for our defense program would be cranted
preference.

As regards the three Japanese vessels now en route
to the United States, you may have noted recently in the
press that the Japanese Government 1s sending these
vessels to this country for the purpose of bringing
passengers, including Americans, from Japan to the
United States and of repatriating Japanese now in the

understanding that these vessels are under requisition
by tho Japanese Government and carry no commercial cargo.
It may be added that the question of obtaining
merchandise from Japan is only a part of a larger problem
involving the whole of our rel ations with Japen, which is
engaging the most earnest cons ideration of this Department
and other agoncies of this Government at the present time,
and that under the circumstances any solution to this
question presents many difficulties and complications.

THE AMEHICAN REPUBLICS
BY
MEMORANDUM FOR THE LIAISON OFFICER, DEPARTMENT OF STATFTMENT OF STATE
894.85/659
Subject: Entry of Tatsuta Maru into Port of
Balboa.
1. In reply to memorandum, Department of State, Division
of Far Eastern Affairs, November 18, 1941, addressed to War
Department - Colonel Bratton, and indorsin a copy of dispatch
No. 1820, Tokyo dated November 18, 6 P.M. I am directed to
inform you as follows:
2. The War Department has no objections to the Tatsuta
Maru calling at Balboa, Canal Zone, as requested by the Japanese
Government. Purchase of supplies from the Panama Canal by
vessels calling at the Canal Zone are habitually made on a cash
basis. In the event that the Japanese Government does not
desire to follow this procedure, it will be necessary for the
State Department or some other agency to make the necessary
arrangements for payment.
3. The War Department in agreeing to the call of this
vessel at Balboa is not committing itself in advance with re-
spect to a possible request that this vessel be permitted to
transit the Canal.
PS/MCS
For the Acting A. C. of S., G-2:
Merimelors

Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: August 26, 1941
APR 2 2 1942
DIVISION OF
Wyndham White of the British Embassy
Mr. J. J. Reinstein of the State Department
FW
COPIES TO:
894.85/672
With reference to Mr. Hall's letter to Mr. Acheson
1—1408
of August 13 regarding the bunkering of Japanese vessels,
I asked what the British policy 1s with regard to the
85
bunkering facilities for Japanese ships in South America.
P
I was told that the policy 1s the same as in British ports,
S
/LMB
894.
subject to the limitations of competition, that is, to
grant bunkers only sufficlent to enable the ship to reach
its next soheduled port of call, or to return home if the
vessel is proceeding to Japan. Mr. White said that this
policy is followed in all ports where the British have a
monopoly on bunkering facilities, and that the British
APR2
are desirous of applying it wherever this can be done.
FILED
Mr. Hall read me a draft letter which he was planning
1941
to send the Department regarding bunkering of Japanese
vessels

=== Page 223 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
vessels 1n South America. The letter stated that there
are some eighteen vessels in the vicinity of the Straits
have teen
which wil he picking up strategic materials which the
British would like to seize. They are very anxious that
these vessels be delayed in South American ports as long
as possible. They would like to work out some coordinated
policy regarding the bunkering of Japanese ships in South
America which would restrict bunker supplies as much as
possible.
1.
J. J. Reinstein

SUBJECT:

STRICTLY CONFIDENTIAN - LIAISON OFFICE
Copcico 2o war-

90 PF5-1942 812
The Honorable
vc.m@
The Secretary DEPMENT OF STATE
SEP 1 - 1942
Washington, D.C.
Sir:
I have the honor to enclose for the Department's
information two copies of British Survey, Vol. IV,
No. 2 (July 31, 1942) dealing with the Japanese merchant
FILED
marine.
291942
Although there is little new information in this
article it is believed to be a good brief summary of
the known facts.
ondon
With reference to several points the article raised
questions which it was pheught advisable to investigate.
OCT 1 6 1942
Thus the language of the third paragraph of page 6
implies that Japan's shipbuilding capacity may be con-
cd
siderably in excess of 500,000 tons per annum, and that
Japanese yards use welded construction and assembly line
methods, although admitting that curtailed supplies br
raw/

=== Page 248 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2-
raw materials might have a limiting effect.
On these points the Embassy interviewed Mr. A.C.
Hardy, a journalist shipping expert now conneoted with
the Admiralty, who supplied the factual information for
the article. Mr. Hardy states that the writers of the
article have misinterpreted his views; that so far as
known the probable maximum capacity of Japanese yards
is about 500,000 tons per annum exclusive of naval
construction; that they do not, so far as can be dis-
covered, use assembly line methods; but that they may
possibly use welded construction.
Mr. Hardy kindly arranged an interview with
representatives of Lloyd's Register of Shipping for a
discussion of the same subject.
Their conclusions in
general coincide with those of kr. Hardy, but they have
promised to provide more detailed information later.
Respectfully yours,
For the Ambassador:
James Somerville

The Imperial Ministry of Foreign Affairs presents

with an agreement recently reached between the Govern-
ment of Japan and the United States concerning the

=== Page 271 === (confidence 0.99)
Translation.
Ministry of Communications,
November , 1941.
REQUISITION ORDER
To the Nippon Yusen Keisya,
The Imperial Japenese Government hereby
requisitions the M.. TATUTA MARU of the Nippon
Yusen Kaisya with the view of transporting passen-
gers and mails between Japan and Los Angeles and
Balboa.
The Imperial Japanese Government is to operate
the said ship from Yokohama to Los Angeles and
Balboa in accordance with the schedule appended to
this order.
Let it be noted that the Nippon Yusen Keisya
shall, on behalf of the Imperial Japanese Govern-
ment, conduct those business matters which may
arise in regard to the afore-mentioned voyage,
under the direction of supervisors who are to em-
bark the TATUTA MARU by order of the Government
and in conformity with the following stipulations:
(1) Whenever a change of the schedule prescrib-
ed by the Government is required due to any special
circumstances,

=== Page 272 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
circumstances, the Nippon Yusen Kaisya shall apply
to the Minister for Communications for the permis-
sion of such change.
(2) With regard to the embarkation of passen-
gers, the Nippon Yusen Kaisya shall act under the
direction of the Minister for Communications end
the Imperial Japanese Consulates respectively for
the outbound and return voyares.
(3) No ordinary cargo shall te loaded on the
said ship. In case it is necessary to load any
special cargo, the preceeding paragraph shall apply
correspondingly.
(4) The Nippon Yusen Kaisya shall submit to
the Minister for Communications & detailed state-
ment of income and expenditure at the end of the
voyage.
Ken Terasima,
His Imperial Majesty's
Minister for Communications.

Through the courtesy of Mr. Charles W. Sprey
of Lloyd's Register here, statements regarding the .
use of welding methods of ship construction in Japan
have been obtained from Lloyd's surveyors who served
in Japan as recently as l939. Copies of these
statements, which Mr. Spray requests be treated as
strictly confidential, are enclosed with this despatch.
Mr. Spray states that Lloyd's Register has had
no reliable information regarding ship construction
in Japan since the middle of 1939, prior to the
outbreak of the European war. He is therefore
unable to furnish later information. However,
the statements enclosed herewith indicate considereble
Japanese interest in welding methods of ship con-
struction as early as 1935 and 1t is considered not
unlikely that such methods are in widespread use now.
With reference to the question of the use of
methods of pre-febrication and assembly, attention
is drewn to the Ministry of Economic Warfare's
notes of Economic Intelligence for the week ending
May 8, 1942 where (Item 27) mention 1s made of
Japenese press reports of September 194l relating
to the standardization of shipping designs in Japan.
Mr. Spray, elthough not a technical expert,
believes that the shipbuilding capacity of Japanese
yards might be increased as much as 20% by the
adoption of welded construction and assembly line
methods, provided of course, that there are no
bottlenecks of material or labor.
Respectfully yours,
For the Ambassador:
James Somerville
Liaison Officer with the
Ministry of Economic Warfare.

CONFIDENTIAL
Statement re use of electric welding for
ship construction in Japan; also re possible
use of essembly line methods and pre-
fabrication of parts for mass production,
by Surveyors to Lloyd's Register.
Surveyor
(service in
Jepan)
Remarks
A.McGlashan
(1929-1939)
"I have no evidence to guide me regarding
Jepanese shipbuilding since the beginning of the
war, and in consequence the following views are
based on experience of their shipyards, elso a
few of their constructionel engineering plants
in the Tokio District before 1939.
"Presuming thet these works geve up building
bridges, sewerage plants, ges end oil containers,
etc., there would be a considerable amount of
skilied labor and plant availeble for febricating
ship materiel, provided they had steel plates and
sections.
"As to assembly at the yards, I am inclined
to think that the ordinary riveted work would be
used, but we cunnot be sure of this because in
1939 electric welding was extensively used in
the larger shipyards. In 1937, Yokohama Dook
Co. had an automatic welding machine made by
the General Eleotrio Co., and no doubt could
make these machines themselves today.
"Every year since l935 electric welders
were being treined in large numbers, and this
praotioe hes probably continued since the war
began."
H.A.Garnett
"When I left Japan about the middle of
(1928-1934)
1934, the employment of welding was fast
becoming the practice for smell ship scantlings.
During 1932, whilst attending duties at Osaka
Iron Works, Sakurajima, a large size all-welded
launch was made for the Japanese Navy.
"Welding classes were also held for the
students or apprentices in the shipyards, and
I have no doubt in my own mind that further
experience hes been gained with welding in
Japanese Shipyards and that all-welded sections
are now being produced.
"I have no knowledge of assembly line
methods or pre-fabrication of ship parts for
mass/

=== Page 293 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
mass production."
H.D. Buchanan
"All shipbuilding and repair yards, which
(1920-1939)
came under my observation, had electric welding
equipment installed.
"I have no knowledge of any all-welded
merchant vessels having been constructed, but
I have seen several 8,000 - 10,000 ton Cruisers
which were of partly welded construction, for
instance, about l60 feet from stem aft was all-
welded construction, and I have no doubt but
that a considerable portion of the remainder
was also welded.
"Regarding assembly line and pre-fabrication
of parts for mass production of ships, I have no
knowledge of any suoh plants."
(signed) C.W. Spray

•SHIPPING CONDITIONS
Depresslon in Deep Sea Shipping Becomen Aoute
Due to a combinantion of oiroumstanoes suoh as the
business reoession in Amerioa, diminshing cargoes both on
the outbound and homeward voyages, boyoott movements in various
countries of the world, domestio exohange oontrol and import
restriotions and rising costs of operating ships, the Japanese
shipping industry is at present undergoing a rather severe
depression. It is reported that on nearly all the services
cargoes have dropped considerably and praotioally all vessels
are carrying only about 50 per cent of their capacities. Only
on the Indian service are oargoes holding up, vessels on this
run reporting 80 per cent, due mainly to oontinued imports
of ootton and iron ore. Japanese shipping companies operating
on the South Amerioan service, which 1s renorted to be the worst
hit of all the lines, are said to be suffering losses and there-
fore are seriously considering reducing the number of sailings
and plaoing their ships in tramp servioe.
In this conneotion it is learned from a reliable source
that operating costs on Japanese vessels have risen approx-
imately 30 per cent during the past year. The present operating
costs are estimated at ¥2.00 per deadweight ton per month
for diesel ships and ¥l.70 for steamships. These figures
do not 1nolude fuel and depreoiation costs. The differenee

=== Page 300 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
ween the costs for the diesel and the steam operated ships
1s attributed to smaller wage and insuranoe costs, lower
valuations for the steamehips resulting in considerably smaller
insuranoe premiums. A representative of a large Japanese
steamship company also stated thst operating costs in the
Japanese merohant marine are substantially higher than most
foreigners are inclined to believe, wages in partioular being
comparatively high due to the compulsion of hiring only Japanese
seamen on ships of Japanese registry instead of being able to
hire cheaper labor as is done in the case of certain European
merobant marines.
With the adveree oonditione showing no signs of mitigating
both governmental and private quarters are conoerned over the
situation and are seeking means to cope with the sharp drop
in cargoes, one suggention being thst of complete control of
shipping distribution. *

*SHIPPING POLICY
Gingle Shippina Firm for China Sarvicee Planned
During the latter part of the month under review, the
press mentioned several times the inoreasing possibility of the
establishment of a single shipping company to operate all China-
Japan services under the Japanese flag, as well as the China
coastal servioes under Japanese registry. That some progress
on the projeot bad been made was indicated by the announeement
of the Department of Communications of its decision to invite
executives of the Nippon Yusen Kaisha, the Osaka Shoson Kaisha
and other interested firms to a conferonce to be held sometime
in May to disouss the establishment of a large firm to operate
the Chime serviees.

coneern w111 be between ¥150,000,000 and ¥200,000,000, the greater

• • •SHIPPING POLICY
Strioter Government Control of anipping Forecant
Endeavoring to bring about simultaneously a reduotion in
freight and oharter rates and a reduotion in the foreign tonnage
under Japanese oharter, the Government found it necessary during
the month to bring direot pressure to bear on shipowners and
operators by a threat of direct governmental oontrol of shipping.
Aotually, legal provisions exist for the control of shipping
in the Ship Control Law, sponsored by Communioatione Minister,
Ryutaro Nagal, shortly after the inoeption of military operations
in China. In practice, however, the control of freights, aharter
rates and the supply or tonnage have rested in the hands of
the shipowners as characterized by the voluntary Shipping Control
Committee (Kalun Jioh1 Tose1 Iinka1), a Joint committee of the
Autonomous Shipping Federation (Kaiun Jiohi Renme1)--a union
of the seven most important shipping companies--and the Japan
Shipowners' Association (Nippon Senshu Xyokai).
The owners, through their various committees and assoo-
1ations, have nominally exeroised control of charter and freight
rates, but it has been found in praotice that aotual rates have
tended to exoeed the standard ones established by the committee
and the Communioations Ministry has made it quite plain that
hess control is exeroised in fact as well as theory the Goverm-
ment itself will have to step in. sinoe neither the Autonomous

=== Page 308 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
Bration nor the Shipowners' Assooiation, nor both together
control all shipowners in the country, the primary line of aotion
will be to enlarge the membership of these assoolations to inolude
all shipowners, even those who own only ships of less than 500
tons.
It may be said in Justification of the shipowners--at least
those comprising the seven prinoipal operatifi--that operation
of their overseas vessels in nearsea service is not partioularly
profitable and cannot offer them enough to warrant withdrawal
of those vessels from overseas service at present oharter and
freight rates.
Nonetheless, the Governnent feele thet more tonnage should
be availsble at a reasonable rate for immediate needs to and
from China and is prepared to exercise as much control as possible
to insure an equitable distribution of tonnage and an equitable
price for immediate national needs.
The government plan for correoting the situation calls
for five general points as follows:
1. Reorganization and enlargement of the Japan Ship-
owners' Association (and incidentally other shipowning
bodiee).
2. Establishment of a shipping guild.
3. Establishment of a shipbuilding law.
4. Revival of subsidy for ocean-going servioes.
5. Enlargement (expansion) of shipping finanoes.
So far as is known, no real agreement between shipowners

=== Page 309 === (confidence 0.96)
-8
the Government was reaahed by the end of the month, though
Nr. Shozo Murata, president of the Osaka Shosen Kaisha and both
shipowners' organizstions promised reorganization to inolude more
members, and probable removal of headquarters to Tokyo to insure
oloser co-operation with the Communioations Ministry.* " * * •
Charter Market
Rates for time oharters were well maintained, and undoubtedly
would have been higher, were it not for the pres ure brought
to bear by the Government to prevent pr fiteering. Up to now,
no adoquate voluntary control of oharter and freight rates could

Governsent Contrel of Shippins Mearer
Last month, it was reported that direot Government control
of shipping appeared likely in view of the in bility of autonomous
shipping bodies to keep freight rates within reason and at the same
time to maintain a satlafaotory volume of avallable tonnage. At
the end of the month under review, direot oontrol appeared elmost
a oertainty.
At the beginning of August, the control bodies met and
decided upon slight reductions in freight and oharter rates. These,
however, do not appear to have satisfied the tovernment, as the
drafting of a shipping guild law and plans for the enlargoment of
the functions and personnel of the Mercantile Marine Pureau of
the Ministry of Communioations oontinued unabated.
The ship-ownere, on their side, state thst they are doing
all they reasonably can to meet the Government's demands. Those
controlled by the Voluntary Control Committee have redueed freight
rates slightly; they have inoreased their memberanip to 139, covering
a total tonnage of 4, 196, 733 (there are, according to the Depart-
ment of Communicat1ons, 1,136 vessels over 1,000 tons, aggrogating
4,753, 266 tons, which should be subjeot to oontrol); they have
broadened the basis of memberahip to include ownere of ships of
100 tons, the recent minimum having been s00 tons; and they have
established a disciplinary oommittee to punish violators of the
Standard freight and charter rates, requesting the Government to

up the committee's decisions within the framework of existing
The Government, on the other hand, appears to feel that the
memberahip of the Shipping Voluntary Control Committee does not
wield suffiolent weight to control all ship-ownere and that those
operating in uoastal waters, in partioular, have evaded, and are
evading the standard rates, thrt tonnage cannot under present
conditions be ordered frm one route to another, as needed, with
sufficlent dispatoh to ellminate the need of chartering foreign
bottoms, a prooess that involves the payment of foreign exchange,
whioh the  overnment is extromely anxious to avoid.
No matter what oomnlaints the Government may have to offer
with regaru to the failurs of the shinning interests to meet its
demands, it seeme reas nably certain that the shipping interests
will not consider themselves in n position to meet those demands
and avoid what in normal tines would be considered flagrant profit

Government Control
After months of study and investigation during whioh sime
numerous plans for the control of shipping in the Empire were
proposed and disoarded, the Cabinet on August 25 approved the
plan drawn up and prerented by the Ministry of Communications.
This plan, which was expeoted to go into effeot September first,
1noludes all phases of both the shipbuilding and ahipping in-
dustries and if striotly enforoed may well result in complete
control by the Government of both polioies and operations of
the firms engaged therein.
Two organs are to be established to carry out the measures
of oontrol outlined in the plan, a shipping ontrol Commission
and a Shipbuilding Reguletion Coámissin, one for each of the
two industries. Members of the commissions will be seleoted
from both offioial and private oiroles.
The duties of the Shipbuilding Regulation Commission shall
be to examine all blue-printe and plans for the construotion of
new vessels over 500 tons or over 160 feet in length and after
aseertaining that they conform to the standards set by the
Ministry of communioations, shall determine in what order the
proposed vessels are to be built. Final permission for cons-
truetion is, however, left to the Ministry of Communications
although the Commiesion 1s delegated to enforce this permit

=== Page 356 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
Systom.
The work mapped out for the Shipping Control Commission
is far broader in scope that that of its sister organisation,
dealing as it does with the complex problems of the shipping
industry. The following is a list of the powers granted this
Commission.
1. To examine all deals which involve more than
50,000 tons of cargo or which will require more
than three monthe to fulfill.
2. To set up and supervise a committee for the
transport of necessary materials for whose transport
bids have not been received.
3. To allot shipping space.
4. To control long distance shipping.
5. To determine and anncunce cargo and oharterage rates.
6. To examine shipping and charterage oontracts.
7. To take necessary measures for the prevention of
control violations.
8. To investigate and oontrol violations and to punish
violators.
The partioulare which heve been published regarding the
new plan are general in soope and working details have not yet
been made publio. These wil! probably be worked out by the
Commissions after they have had ample opportunity to investigate
the various problems.
Just a few days after the announoement that the Cabinet
had approved the new plan the Japan Shipowners' Assooiation held
a gemeral meeting for the purpose of requesting the membere of
the assoolation to oooperate with the Government in the exeeutiom
of the proposed control. The reaotion of the members was not
announeed.

CONTROL OF OCEAN FREIOHT RATER
With the expansion of the netion's produotive oapacity,
facilities for the transportation of major eommodities have
acquired paramount importance and the inadequaey of the trans-
portation system as a whole has now been taken up as a conditiem
that requires serious consideration from the standpoint of adjusting
commodity prices. The "outline Plan for Prioe Control" which
the Cabinet rdopted on May 5 embodies, as might have been ex-
peeted, a set of regulstions for the oontrol of transportation
costs and of wages, business profits, house and land rentals as
the main constituent factors of the costs. Even before the
China hostilities, ooean transportation oharges had risen sub-
stantially owing to a general improvement of the world shipping
market, espeolally th: t of nearsem maintained a bullish tone
in spite of a depression in deepsea market. The effeot on
commodity prices has therefore received due consideration. On
July 1, l937, seven leading ahipning onmpanies 1noluding the
Nippon Yusen Kaisha and the Osaka Shosen Kaisha, formed what
was afterward oalled the shipping Autonomous League to meet
eventual developments. Almost immediately the shipping
business became inoreasingly diffioult due to the outbreak
of hostilities in hain, the requisitioning of military
transports, and the rise of shipping and other costs.
Because of the subsldies paid by the Government, freight
oharges of regul r services cannot be ohanged without official
approval and the oper: tion of the business generally through
cartels requires contracts between consigners and ship operators
whioh also prevent the free raising of rates in response to the
market's movements. Tramp service, however, is not subjeoted to
these conditions and as shipment contracts are on a short term
basis, the freight market rises sharply when the supply of
bottoms is low, as at present. Therefore the polioy of freight
rate control is centered on tramp service. Furthermore the
freight carried by regular services consists largely of fiber gooks
and miseellaneous products whioh move regularly and periodically
while the tramp vessels onrry bulk cargoes such as iron ore,
coal, and lumber and their freight rates on these sommodisies
are high in proportion to their value. This is especially
true of iron ore and pig trom. On the high grade iron ore from
Lamgyen, which ls within fairly easy reash, the rail freight from
the mime to Taku 1s 3.50 and the ceeam freight from Taku to
Yawmsa 1s *7.00 or twiee as mueh. of the c.1.f. cost of 23.80
at Yawate, the rail freightage represents 14.7 per eent and the

=== Page 358 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
San freightage 29.4 per cent. At Tayaoshan in Cemtral Chimm
me eosts of mining and shipment are extremely low, and the priee
of Zungun ore at the mine is also low, but the ooean freightage
in both cases represents 50 per cent of the cost. The proportion
for Indian pig iron 1s even higher at 58.6 per cent.
A similar condition exists for coal. The price of Hokkaide
coal delivered at Yokohama or Tokyo 1s given by the Showa Coal
Company (a sales syndicate) at x2l.39, of whioh the osean freight
represente 30.3 per oent. The freight oharge on Kyushu eoal
le lower because shipping operation conditions are better, the
proportion being 23.4 per cent of the price at Tokyo or Yokohama
and l8.5 per cent of that at Kobe or Osaka. Ooean transportation
charges on coal from Chunghsing and Kailuan make up about s0
per oent of the cost.
The proportion of freight charges 1s of course higher when
cargo is carried over long distanoes. Industrial salt, for
instance, comes from three sources of supply that can be
olassified as nearsea, quasi-distant sea, and distant sea, whieh
are represented respeotively by Chunglu or North China selt, Java
salt and Afrioan salt. The respective ratios of freight eharges
to delivered cost are 38.8%, 52.4% and 73%. In the last instanee
about three-quarters of the cost consists of freightage, and even
though the proportion 1s reduced t: 68.7 when the oarriere are
foreign vessels whose rates ar- lower, it is not surprising that
coneumers suoh as the manufacturere of alkali products and glass
are seriously conoerned.
The proportion of the freightage 1s 26 for North Saghalion
lumber as against 52.9% for Bouth Sea lumber. It also varies
according to the kind of produot, thet for large and medium
quares of Oregon pine being 35.9§, and that for lower prioed
uchtkonl long logs being as high as about 67%.
As trampers generally carry low prioed commodities in bulk,
the freightage constitutes a higher peroentage of the cost, while
the ratio for commodities carried by regular liners is relatively
lower because the cargoes ere more exnensive and the freight rates
are restricted. The ratios for Indian cotton and Ameriean cottom
are resepotively 4.1 and 3.8%, and those for Australian and South
African wool are as low as 2.1 and 2.2 respeotively.
It is evident that the ourrent freight rates in tramp
service are an important element in the price struoture of im-
ported commodities. Their peroentages have been held in aheek
to some extent sinoe the China trouble, by means of autonomous
measures taken by the ship operators; but oompared with Jume
1987 both the freight rates themselves and their ratio to the
prices of commodities are considerably higher.

seseed to any appresiable extent, for the volume of earge

oonduoted, but it was deoided that new international and China

neeessitated the invocation of the Mobilisation Law.

the entire range of the marine traneportation industry through
the granting of large powers to sunervise and license shipbuilding
and ship repairs, direct the use and assignment of vessels, and

of eargo to be transported, the same Ministry may designate,
limit, or prohibit at its disoretion.

Transportation Control Ordinanoe, 1t appears inevitable that

SHIPPING POLICY
Bingle Government Combine for All shina
During August, after previous rumors of an extensive re-
organisation of Japanese mhipping control polioy, following a
cabinet meeting the Government announced 1ts plan for unifying
control and operation of all ships under a single judioial entity
to be accomplished by 1nvoking Artiole l8 of the National General
Mobilisation Law. The Central Marine Transportation Control
Association, whioh itself was formed only several months ago, as
the shipping oontrol agenoy in cooperation with the Government,
will be dissolved by the new plan.
From the details available the Government has deoided upon
state control and management of all shipping, because of the
1nereasing tension in the international field. To that end the
plan is divided into three parts, dealing with the management of
shipping, seamen and shipbuilding through the fortheoming central
organ. Requisitioning of vessels would be under Artiele lo of
the National Mobilisation Law, while seamen would be requisitioned
under Artiole 4 of the same law.
Far-reaching in its effeots and drastic in the changes it
1mposes on the shipping industry, the Government plan was outlined
in a statement made by the Communications Ministry that gives the
best summary of all the measures contemplated and accordingly is
appended to this report as Appendix A.

FEB 2 1943
My dear Mr. Secretary:
The receipt is acknowledged of your letter of January 13,
1943 (IN 894.85/689), in which you refer to the Department's
letter of November 12, 1942, and you enclose a copy of a note
from the Netherlands Ambassador, dated December 17, 1942, fur-
ther in regard to his request for information oomeerning the
disposal of the cargo of the Japanese vessel SANYO MARU, which
was discharged at the port of New York, N. Y., on or about May
27, 1940.
The Department's letter of November 12, 1942, states that
the record before the Department failed to establish that the
Netherlands Shipping and Trading Committee is a party in in-
terest entitling it to the confidential information contained
in the official documents filed in the collector's office which
relate to the cargo, within the purview of the customs regula-
tions relating thereto. It was suggested that final action
would be withheld pending the receipt of further date which
would determine whether the information could be furnished con-
sistently.
The note in reference advises that the Netherlands Shipping
and Trading Committee acts as oustodian for the cargo on the
vessel by virtue of the power vested in it by the Royal Nether-
Lands Govermment, temporarily residing in London.
Pursuant to the request of the Netherlands Ambassador, there
is enelosed a oopy of the report of the supervising custems agent,
Now Yerk, . I., relating to the disposition whieh was made of the
cargo in question.
By direetion of the Secretary:
Very truly yours,
(Signed) Horer E (Go:on
HERBERT E. GASTON,
Assistant Seoretary ef the Treasury.

6
Referring to your letter of May 7, 1943 you are
informed that the American Embassy in Tokyo reported
in October 1941 that it was imposs1ble for the Embassy
or the American Consular Officers in Japan to obtain
definite information as to the Japanese vessols req-
uisitioned or otherwise diverted from carrying cargo to
the United States. The Embassy stated however that
various Consular Officers in Japan had obtained the
following information: Bordeaux Maru, Nako Maru,
Asamasan Maru, and Kongo Maru reportedly requisitioned;
Kimikawa Maru, Kofyu Maru, and Nankai Maru, presumably
requisitioned; Nako Maru and Asara Maru diverted while
en route from Japan to San Francisco.
The Embassy has furnished the following explanation
of the procedure by which the movements and disposition
of Japanese ships are controlled officially:
The Government, since the beginning of this year, 194l,
has employed the Central Marine Transportation Control
Association to direct the assignment of ships. wide
powers as a state agency are possessed by this Association
and it funotions in collaboration with the Ministry of
Communications under the National Commodity Mobilization
P
Ordinances. In addition to this means of control the
S
Ships Protection Law which came into effeet on April 20, 1941
/MJN
gave authority to the Ministry of the Navy to issue
Instruotions as to navigation, et cetere.
It is not known whether the ships which were diverted
during the course of their voyage to the United States
returned

returned to Japan by formal order of the Government.
Instruetions or mere requests by the Ministry of the
Navy, Communications Ministry or the Central Association
presumably would serve the same purpose.

the Nethethands Ambassador presents his compliments

In the Department of State's note of February 12,

course, certainty on this point does not exist.

use of blocks and tackles and cables linked with the
shore.

=== Page 477 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF COMMERCE
BUREAU OF
MARINE INSPECTION AND NAVIGATION
1
WASHINGTON
102.2-2
ASSISTANT TO THE
December 12, 1940
11940
MEMORANDUM
COUNSELOR
IN REPLY REFER TO
To:
Mr. Carlton Savage,
State Department.
From: Acting Assistant Director, Bureau of Marine Inspection
894.85/12-1240
and Navigation.
Attached are copies of correspondence transmitted to
m by the Commissioner of Customs. It is to be noted that Japanese
naval tankers and a Japanese naval cargo boat are due to arrive in
San Francisco in the near future, if they are not already there.
94.
One of the tankers is said to have on board nine boxes of printed
matter for delivery in the United States, and the Commissioner of
Customs has instructed the collector to treat that printed matter
as any other import would be treated. It is expected that the
vessels will take on cargoes of oil presumably for the use of the
Japanese navy. We must inform the collector whether export
declarations and ships' manifests are to be required for these
vessels w ich are to take on the oil cargoes, and also whether
the vessels should make entry upon arrival and be required to
clear prior to departure.
Section 441 of the Tariff Act of 1930 (19 U.S.C. 1441)
exempts from the requirement of making entry "vessels of war and
public vessels employed for the convenience of letters and dispatches
and not permitted by the laws of the nations to which they belong
to be employed in the transportation of passengers or merchandise
in trade".
It seems that the one vessel which is bringing in the
printed matter is engaging in trade and might be required to make
entry at the custom house. It might also be considered that the
894.85
vessels which are to take on board cargoes of oil likewise are
engaging in trade. However, if this view is taken and entry and
clearance of the vessels is to be required, it may result in
embarrassment to the United States Navy in obtaining fuel and
supplies for the fleet in foreign ports, as such a requirement
may be taken as a precedent by other nations. It would seem,
40
-1248

=== Page 478 === (confidence 0.97)
Pas 2
Mr. Savage
however, that whether entry and clearance are required or not, it
would be desirable that export declarations be filed for the oil
so laden in order that the statistical records should truly reflect
the quantities of oil leaving the United States.
I will appreciate an expression of your views as to the
action to be taken in this case bearing in mind that under our
laws we can probably justify any action which may be taken.
Acting Assistant Director
